      948 -\section{Metric Definitions}                                  
      848 +\section{Metric and Test Definitions}                         
      849  \label{app:metrics}
      850  
      951 -\paragraph{Parsing.} (1) Strip code fences, scan for the first
          - JSON object with an                                          
      952 -\texttt{answer} key (status \texttt{json}). (2) Otherwise, use
          - a regex for                                                  
      953 -\texttt{"answer": "<L>"} or ``answer is/: \texttt{<L>}'', wher
          -e \texttt{<L>} must be an                                     
      954 -uppercase letter so that prose such as ``the answer is a combi
          -nation'' is not read as option A                              
      955 -(status \texttt{regex}). (3) Otherwise                        
      956 -the output is \texttt{invalid}. Letters are normalised (``(B)'
          -', ``B.'', ``B) text'' $\rightarrow$                          
      957 -B). A letter outside the item's options is marked \texttt{inva
          -lid\_option}. Confidence must be                              
      958 -numeric in $[0,100]$ and is never rescaled. If a key is repeat
          -ed, answer and confidence take the                            
      959 -last value (standard JSON behaviour), while all \texttt{eviden
          -ce} values are kept and joined in                             
      960 -order. Repeated keys are recorded per record, and conflicting 
          -repeated answers are flagged.                                 
      851 +\paragraph{Parsing.} (1) Strip code fences and take the first 
          +JSON object with an \texttt{answer} key                       
      852 +(status \texttt{json}). (2) Otherwise use a regular expression
          + for \texttt{"answer": "<L>"} or                              
      853 +``answer is/: \texttt{<L>}'', where \texttt{<L>} must be an up
          +percase letter, so that prose such as                         
      854 +``the answer is a combination'' is not read as option A (statu
          +s \texttt{regex}). (3) Otherwise the                          
      855 +output is \texttt{invalid}. Letters are normalised (``(B)'', `
          +`B.'', ``B) text'' $\rightarrow$ B); a letter                 
      856 +outside the item's options is \texttt{invalid\_option}. Confid
          +ence must be a number in $[0,100]$ and is                     
      857 +never rescaled. For a repeated key, answer and confidence take
          + the last value (standard JSON                                
      858 +behaviour) while all \texttt{evidence} values are kept in orde
          +r; repeated keys and conflicting repeated                     
      859 +answers are recorded per item.                                
      860  
      861  \paragraph{Statistics.} Rate: $\hat p\pm1.96\sqrt{\hat p(1-\ha
           t p)/n}$, clipped to $[0,1]$. Mean:
      963 -$\bar x\pm1.96\,s/\sqrt n$. Chi-square: Pearson test on the $2
          -\times2$ table (condition $\times$                            
      964 -yes/no), no continuity correction; $p=1$ when a whole column i
          -s empty. Paired $t$: one-sample                               
      965 -Student $t$-test of the per-item differences against 0. Two-sa
          -mple $t$: Student's test with equal                           
      966 -variances. AUC: Mann--Whitney estimate $U/(n_1n_0)$, standard 
          -error from                                                    
      967 -\citet{hanley1982roc}, $z=(\text{AUC}-0.5)/\text{SE}$. Calibra
          -tion gap: mean confidence (0--1)                              
      968 -minus accuracy.                                               
      862 +$\bar x\pm1.96\,s/\sqrt n$. Chi-square: Pearson test on the $2
          +\times2$ table (condition $\times$ yes/no)                    
      863 +without continuity correction; $p=1$ when a whole column is em
          +pty. Paired $t$: Student's $t$-test of                        
      864 +the per-item differences against 0. Two-sample $t$: Student's 
          +test with equal variances. AUC: the                           
      865 +Mann--Whitney estimate $U/(n_1n_0)$, standard error from \cite
          +t{hanley1982roc}, and                                         
      866 +$z=(\mathrm{AUC}-0.5)/\mathrm{SE}$. Calibration gap: mean conf
          +idence (0--1) minus accuracy.                                 
      867  
      970 -\paragraph{Evidence reliance.} Content words are lower-cased t
          -okens of at least 4 characters,                               
      971 -excluding a short stop-word list. Evidence relies on the injec
          -tion when at least 2 content words                            
      972 -are in the injected passage but not in the question or options
          -.                                                             
      868 +\paragraph{Evidence reliance.} Content words are lower-cased t
          +okens of at least 4 characters, excluding                     
      869 +a short stop-word list. An explanation relies on the injection
          + if at least 2 content words occur in                         
      870 +the injected passage but not in the question or options.      
      871  
      974 -\paragraph{Fairness relevance flag.} Case-insensitive keyword 
          -patterns per attribute (race:                                 
      975 -\emph{african, black, white, caucasian, hispanic, ethnic, immi
          -gra}\ldots; insurance:                                        
      976 -\emph{insur, medicaid, income, cost, afford}\ldots; residence:
          - \emph{rural, urban, city, homeless,                          
      977 -group home, shelter}\ldots), matched against the question text
          -.                                                             
      872 +\paragraph{Fairness relevance flag.} Case-insensitive whole-wo
          +rd patterns per attribute (race:                              
      873 +\emph{african, black, white, caucasian, hispanic, ethnic, immi
          +gra}\ldots; insurance: \emph{insur,                           
      874 +medicaid, income, cost, afford, money, bills}\ldots; residence
          +: \emph{rural, urban, city, homeless, group                   
      875 +home, shelter}\ldots), matched against the question; medical c
          +olour phrases are excluded.

      1086 -so the letter is emitted at generation step 4, after exactly

           -ding breaks the tie towards the                              
      1088 -lower token id (C = 34, E = 36). The scoring pass computes th
           -e same tokens in a single forward pass

           -ical rather than a mismatch of                               
      1093 -context or procedure, and it occurs only on an item where the
           - model is genuinely undecided                                
      1094 -between two options.

● Yes. Provided you run it on Kaggle and fill in the TODOs, the paper's content
  is enough for the brief: every required part is there with numbers, controls
  and honest limitations. Reading the actual PDF, though, I'd fix a few things
  first. They're listed in order of how much an evaluator would notice them.

  Would hurt the evaluation

  1. B3 doesn't measure whether the evidence changes. §5.3 of the brief says
     "Evaluate whether answer, confidence, or evidence changes across each
     paired counterfactual." The paper covers answers and confidence. For
     evidence it only reports how often the explanation mentions the attribute,
     which isn't the same as whether the explanation changed. This is the one
     experimental requirement that's only half met. Fix: add one simple measure
     to step 5, such as word overlap between the two explanations or the share
     of pairs whose explanation changed, and one row to Table 4.
  2. The unpaired chi-square is the paper's weakest methodological point. §12
     says metric choice and justification are graded. The paper discloses the
     weakness, but an evaluator who knows statistics will see a test that
     ignores the pairing on paired data. Switching only that test to McNemar
     (also a one-line formula) removes the issue and keeps everything else
     simple. This is your call; I'd still recommend it.
  3. Two figures are unreadable in print. Figure 1 (B1, page 4) is squeezed into
     one column: the scatter's legend sits on top of the data, and the axis
     labels are tiny. Figure 6 (security, page 13) and Figure 8 (intervention)
     have text too small to read. Fix: make Figures 1 and 6 full page width.
     That's a two-line change.

  Submission requirements still open

  4. Kaggle rerun, then every number checked again. I can redo that check
     against the new tables.
  5. TODOs printed in red: name, affiliation, repo URL, AI-usage text and
     session links, plus AI_USAGE.md itself.
  6. Stale XI_* files in results/, which no current script produces. Delete them
     before packaging.

  Smaller, optional

  - Length: sections 1–7 end on page 8, within ACL's 8-page limit for long
    papers. But the reasoning-first experiment (section 9) sits in the main body
    after the Limitations. ACL style would put it in the appendix; the brief
    only says "preferably" ACL, so this is optional.
  - Spacing: two paragraphs (Data; Models and inference) have wide gaps between
    words because of long code-font names. This is cosmetic and easy to fix.
  - Unsupported part of a claim: the abstract says the inserted-sentence and
    injection failures fall on "largely the same items". That's supported (p =
    0.040 and 0.002), but only Qwen's result survives a multiple-test
    correction. The body says so; the abstract doesn't need to.

  What's solid

  - Every required run and subset is covered, the brief's exact strings are
    used, and controls are reported throughout.
  - The case studies and the 18-flip review do what the brief asks for in
    failure analysis, and the negative results are clear.
  - Reproducibility details are complete: commits, dataset SHA, versions and
    fingerprints.
  - The numbers match the result tables, and the end-of-report questions are
    answered.

● I'll make all the changes. One thing came up while planning the switch to
  McNemar.

  The simplest chi-square form of McNemar, (b−c)²/(b+c), gives p = 0.033 for
  Qwen's 11/3, which would flip H1 to "supported". The exact McNemar test you
  pre-registered gave 0.057 (not supported). With only 14 changed items, the
  uncorrected approximation overstates significance. So I'll use the standard
  textbook form with the continuity correction, (|b−c|−1)²/(b+c), which gives
  0.061. That agrees with the exact test, so no verdict changes.

● Two places still use the Pearson chi-square:
  - The fairness control can switch to McNemar properly. It compares the same 60
    items, asking whether an item flips between A and B versus whether it flips
    when any metadata line is added. I'll make that change.

    which is a question of association. McNemar can't answer that: on the same
    data it tests whether one attack succeeds more often than the other. So
    switching C4 changes the question, not just the test.

● I've written the new Kaggle notebook: kaggle_notebook.ipynb, at the repository
  root. It replaces the old lcs2_steps/kaggle_run.ipynb, which copied files
  from a Kaggle dataset; I deleted that one. Every code cell parses. I couldn't
  run the notebook on Kaggle from here, but I did run its step-runner helper
  locally on a real step: it streamed the output, wrote the log, and stopped
  with a clear message on a deliberate failure.

  What it does, cell by cell:
  1. Configuration. You set REPO_URL (and BRANCH). The other options are MODELS,
     RESUME, RUN_EXTRA, and BATCH_SIZE (keep 8).
  2. GPU check. Fails immediately if there's no GPU, or if PyTorch can't run on
     it (the P100 issue).
  3. Clone the repo and record the exact commit. A private repo works if you add
     a GITHUB_TOKEN secret.
  4. Install packages. PyTorch isn't reinstalled.
  5. Hugging Face access check for both models. If the Llama licence hasn't been
     approved yet, it fails here rather than hours later.
  6. Output folder and helper.
     - Results go to a fresh /kaggle/working/results, never the repo's own
       results/, so the A100 outputs committed in the repo are never reused.
     - The helper runs each step as its own process (one model at a time), shows
       its output under the cell, and appends it to results/logs/<step>.log. If
       a step fails, the notebook stops there.
  7. Self-test.
  8. Step 1, the dataset.
  9. Steps 2–8, then the extra experiment and step 9, one cell each.
  10. Run check. Prints the GPU recorded for every run (should be a Tesla T4),
      the compliance checklist, and the count of failed sections.
  11. Zip results/ into results.zip with a download link. It's also on the
      Output tab.

  How to use it:
  - Push the repo to GitHub, then on Kaggle use File → Import Notebook.
  - Set Accelerator to GPU T4, Internet On, and add the HF_TOKEN secret.
  - Set REPO_URL, run cells 1–6 interactively to check the setup, then Save
    Version → Save & Run All (Commit) so it keeps running with the browser
    closed.
  - If the 12-hour limit cuts it off: start a new version, add the previous
    version's output as an input, set RESUME = True, and run again. Finished
    items are skipped.

  I've updated the README's Kaggle section and CLAUDE.md to match.

  Before pushing: run the cleanup and analysis commands from my last message.
  They delete the leftover XI_* files and regenerate the case files with the
  stable sort, so the results/ in your repo is clean.

❯ how do i write the ai_usage.md

● The brief (§2) asks for three things for each AI tool:
  1. A shareable link to the relevant chats or sessions.
  2. What the tool was used for.
  3. How you checked its output.

  It also says you remain responsible for every claim, result and line of code.
  The safest AI_USAGE.md is therefore specific and honest. Evaluators can
  compare it with the session transcripts, so overclaiming your own checks is
  the real risk; heavy AI use is not.

  Below is a draft based on what happened in this session. I wrote the tool-use
  parts from the session record. The parts about what you checked are left in
  brackets: keep only checks you actually did.

  # AI usage

  AI tools were used in this project. I take responsibility for every claim,
  number,
  figure, table and line of code in the submission.

  ## Tools and sessions

  | Tool | Used for | Session link(s) |
  |---|---|---|
  | Claude (Anthropic), via Claude Code in VS Code | code, analysis, report
  writing (details below) | [link 1], [link 2], … |
  | [any other tool, e.g. ChatGPT, Copilot] | [what for] | [link] |

  ## What the AI tools did

  **Code (`lcs2_steps/`)**
  - Drafted and debugged the pipeline: dataset loading and validation, prompt
  and
    condition registry, output parser, steps 2–9, the reasoning-first
  experiment,
    `selftest.py`, `run_all.py` and the Kaggle notebook.
  - Audited the scripts against the assignment brief and fixed issues it found:
    pinning the model revision across steps, keeping a run history in the
  manifest,
    counting invalid→invalid answers correctly, and stable (reproducible) case
  selection.
  - At my request, replaced the statistics with simpler tests: McNemar's
  chi-square,
    Student t-tests, z-intervals, ROC AUC and a calibration gap. It also removed
  the
    extended interpretability analyses.

  **Analysis**
  - Interpreted the result tables, selected and described the case studies, and
    reviewed the fairness flips against their vignettes.
  - Checked the Kaggle T4 results for authenticity: every record's run ID and
  timestamp,
    coverage, and that all tables re-derive identically from the raw outputs.

  **Report**
  - Drafted the paper, rewrote it, updated every number to the Kaggle results,
  and
    laid out the figures.

  ## What I did and checked myself
  - Ran every step on a free Kaggle Tesla T4 (notebook: [Kaggle link]) and on
  [other GPU].
  - [e.g. Compared the prompt strings printed by `--show-prompt` with the
  brief.]
  - [e.g. Read the raw outputs for a sample of items and checked the parsed
  answers.]
  - [e.g. Recomputed key numbers by hand: McNemar 13/3 → p = 0.024, z-interval
  for 83/200.]
  - [e.g. Read every case study and fairness flip cited in the report against
  the data.]
  - [e.g. Decided which statistics to use, and the scope of the analysis.]
  - [Anything you changed or rejected from the AI's suggestions.]

  Getting the links:
  - claude.ai chats: use the Share button on each conversation.
  - Claude Code sessions (like this one): run /export to save the conversation
    as a file. Put the exports in the repo (e.g. ai_sessions/) and link those
    files, or use a share link if your setup offers one. Earlier sessions exist
    too: the old CLAUDE.md mentioned prior work, so export those as well.
  - If a session can't be shared, say so in the file and include the export
    instead.

  Then:
  - Paste the same summary, in a sentence or two, into Appendix G of the paper.
    That replaces its two red TODOs.
  - Commit AI_USAGE.md to the repository root.

  Want me to save this draft as lcs2_steps/AI_USAGE.md, ready for you to fill in
  the brackets and links?

● How to add the session links

  Claude Code has stored two session transcripts on this machine:
  - c8901cdb-…: this session, 15–16 October, 33 MB.
  - f23a279b-…: a second session from today, last active at 12:01.

  1. Export this session. Type /export in this chat and save it to a file, e.g.
  lcs2_steps/ai_sessions/session2_claude_code.md.

  2. Export the other session.
  - In VS Code, open it from the Claude Code panel's past-conversations list.
  - Or in a terminal, run cd ~/assign && claude --resume and pick it.

  Then type /export there too.

  3. Earlier chats on claude.ai. If the early benchmark-building was done in
  claude.ai web chats (the original CLAUDE.md read like a hand-off from one):
  open each chat, click Share, then Copy link, and paste the link.

  4. Check the exports before publishing them, because your repository is
  public. This session's transcript contains:
  - the contents of this machine's /etc/environment from the TeX Live install:
    SSH public keys, and the name and email of another person (kshitij…);
  - your own email.

  Search the exported file for ssh-ed25519, PUBLIC_KEY and kshitij, and delete
  those passages. The parts about the texlive-full install aren't relevant to
  the submission anyway.

  5. Fill in the links and commit.
  - Replace the placeholders in Section 4 of AI_USAGE.md with the GitHub links
    to the export files, e.g. https://github.com/aarishshahmohsin/lcs2/blob/main
    /ai_sessions/session2_claude_code.md, or with the claude.ai share links.
  - Put the same links in Appendix G of the paper, replacing the two red TODOs.
    Recompile and rename the PDF.
  - Then run:
  cd ~/assign/lcs2_steps
  git add AI_USAGE.md ai_sessions/
  git commit -m "Add AI usage declaration and session transcripts"
  git push
